m^{*}_{\rm{sample}}
and
m^{*}_{\rm{dRSB}}
are from Table
2
.
We ran
HPR
for
10,000
steps and record the best trial solution that reached consensus within
p
steps. Results are then averages over 10 runs on graphs of size
n=10,000
.
The values with an asterisk are an upper bound on the
d1RSB
phase, stemming from the last
RS
stable values of
m_{\mathrm{init}}
. Also shown are the minimal values of
m_{\mathrm{init}}
reached by the
SA
procedure. Note that
subzero
values of
m_{\mathrm{init}}
correspond to states where minority becomes majority. Unlike SA,
HPR
is able to explicitly find such states for
d\geq 4,p=3
,
as predicted by the
BDCM
.

Our goal is to find an initialization for a specific instance of a large random graph that attains the lowest possible magnetization, while reaching
+1
consensus within
p
steps. We follow the procedure described in the
HPR
Algorithm
1
, complemented by the dynamic programming implementation in Algorithm
2
, and discuss the impact of replica symmetry breaking.

Table
3
presents the minimal initial magnetization
m^{*}_{\mathrm{HPR}}
obtained using
HPR
for varying degrees
d
and path lengths
p
. To obtain these values, we generate
10
random instances of
d
-RRG with
n=10^{4}
nodes and perform the HPR procedure for each combination of
d
and
p
. The complete
HPR
parameters are listed in Table
8
in Appendix
C.5
.
The resulting initial magnetizations leading to consensus are then averaged. In particular, for
d\geq 4
,
p\geq 3
we observe that
HPR
indeed finds initializations with subzero magnetization: initializations where a minority quickly turns into the majority.

The values achieved by the
HPR
are significantly lower than the values
m^{*}_{\rm{sample}}
. We recall that
m^{*}_{\rm{sample}}
denotes the threshold below which the
+1
consensus stops being the entropically dominating attractor, i.e. the lowest value of
m_{\mathrm{init}}
for which the dynamics ends in the homogeneous attractor when initialized uniformly at random as
n\rightarrow\infty
. This underscores the impact of strategic initialization.
Moreover, as noted in Section
2
, the dynamical difference between strategic and typical initializations can be seen as an Mpemba-like effect. Using
HPR
, we can indeed find such initializations that are “hotter” (farther from the all-ones consensus) but nevertheless reach equilibrium faster than colder, randomly initialized states.

In Table
3
we further compare the
HPR
-obtained initial magnetization
m^{*}_{\mathrm{HPR}}
with those obtained via an alternative algorithmic strategy – simulated annealing (
SA
)
[
KGV83
]
. We call the minimal initial magnetization obtained from
SA

m^{*}_{\mathrm{SA}}
. We describe the
SA
procedure in the context of majority dynamics on
d
-RRGs in Appendix
D
. The
SA
parameters are summarized in Table
9
and, due to slow convergence,
n=3\,000
was used.
Note that, for
p=3
, the
SA
algorithm was unable to reach subzero magnetization initializations, even when the
HPR
was. More extensive comparison (including
p\leq 3
, different
n
s and a check of a longer path to consensus for obtained initializations) of the SA and HPR performances can be found in Appendices
C
,
D
.

The analysis above focuses on solutions whose initial configurations reach full consensus within at most
p
steps. However, both the
HPR
and
SA
algorithms can generate initial configurations that, while not satisfying this constraint, nonetheless converge to consensus quickly, possibly starting from even a lower
m_{\rm init}
. In this sense,
HPR
can serve as a heuristic for identifying configurations that reach consensus after an effective time
T_{\rm eff}>p
, even though the optimization only enforces behavior up to time
p
. We did not explore this direction systematically, since doing so would require controlling the magnetization at time
p
. However, we note that constraining consensus at step
p
alone already yields configurations that ultimately reach consensus at
T_{\rm eff}>p
with initial magnetizations lower than
m^{*}_{\rm HPR}
at time
p
. These additional results are presented in Appendix
C.2
. A similar analysis is conducted for
SA
in Appendix
D
.

The
HPR
approaches but does not reach the
RS
lower bounds
m^{*}_{\rm RS}
(Table
2
).
While tuning the hyperparameters such as
\lambda_{\mathrm{init}}
allows the
HPR
algorithm to reach lower values of
m^{*}_{\rm{HPR}}
at the cost of a larger number of steps (as shown in Figure
11
), these values plateau before reaching
m^{*}_{\rm RS}
. We discuss this next.

1RSB implications on algorithmic performance of
HPR

The Overlap Gap Property (OGP) provides a rigorous explanation for typical-case hardness and has been proven to defeat a broad class of algorithms
[
Gam21
]
. Its key feature is a solution space of small, distant clusters, a concept related to the frozen clusters discussed in Section
4.3
, which are conjectured to contain algorithmically inaccessible solutions
[
ZM08
,
Zde08
,
GMZ22
,
ZM08a
]
. The frozen
d1RSB
phase often signals the onset of this complex geometry. We provide experimental evidence for this framework in a dynamical setting.

The lowest initial magnetization
m^{*}_{\rm{HPR}}
reached by the
HPR
algorithm is, in all considered cases, very close to the
d1RSB
threshold
m^{*}_{\rm{dRSB}}
, as seen in Table
3
. Thus, the
d1RSB
transition seems to mark the limiting performance of the
HPR
algorithm. However, the onset of d1RSB is in general not sufficient to have generic algorithmic hardness
[
Krz+07
]
. In addition, it is postulated that the solutions must be frozen. The study of frozen clusters is discussed in Section
4.3
and expanded upon in Appendix
B.5
. While not conclusive, our numerical results suggest that freezing appears as soon as the
d1RSB
phase is entered. This is consistent with the performance of
HPR
. This also suggests that
m^{*}_{\rm{d1RSB}}
marks the initial magnetization under which initial configurations that go to consensus are hard to find algorithmically in a reasonable time.

7
Conclusion and Future Work

In this work, we investigated how the behavior of complex systems depends on initial conditions, focusing on synchronous discrete-time dynamics on sparse graphs. Specifically, we considered the majority dynamics of binary variables on random
d
-regular graphs. In this setting, we used the
backtracking dynamical cavity method (BDCM)
to obtain lower bounds on the initial magnetizations that lead to
+1
consensus in typical
d
-random regular graphs in the limit where the number of nodes
n
goes to infinity. To find such low magnetization initializations on a given graph, we introduced the
history passing reinforcement (HPR)
algorithm.
HPR
outperforms the simulated annealing approach and reaches initializations where
+1
nodes begin as a minority but, within a few steps, become the consensus for all
d\geq 4
random regular graphs. The existence of such configurations for random
3
-regular graphs is left as an open challenge.

We complement these results with
one-step replica symmetry breaking (1RSB)
calculations that identify the onset of the
dynamical one-step replica symmetry breaking (d1RSB)
phase as an accurate marker of the performance limits of the
HPR
algorithm. In particular, the lowest initial magnetization values achieved by
HPR
consistently remain close to the
d1RSB
threshold. Additionally, the
1RSB
analysis seems to indicate that typical solutions are frozen. Thus, we hypothesize that the
d1RSB
threshold marks the onset of algorithmic hardness for the considered graph ensemble. Our results advance the understanding of belief propagation based reinforcement algorithms and their limitations in the largely uncharted dynamical regime.

The
HPR
algorithm is based on the
BDCM
and thus shares the same limitations. In particular, it is computationally constrained to short trajectories (exponential complexity in the length of trajectories), and so it is also restricted to dynamical systems with short attractors. Additionally, in some settings, we observe a sensitive dependence of the required number of iterations on the hyperparameter values. To achieve optimal performance, fine-tuning of these hyperparameters is needed.

HPR
demonstrably provides insights into the difficult study of non-equilibrium properties in dynamical systems. Therefore, we expect it to help address various combinatorial optimization questions in toy models of such complex phenomena as epidemics, ecology, spin glasses, and social dynamics.

Possible extensions of this work include incorporating dynamical rules that vary across individual nodes. This would lead to more realistic models of social phenomena or various bribing scenarios, where not only the initial values of nodes but also their behavior can be influenced. The effectiveness of
HPR
on other classes of graphs than random regular remains an open question. Thus, another avenue for future research is applying
HPR
to sparse real-world networks or graphs that evolve over time. Additionally, an important extension would be to consider more than two possible node values. Furthermore, we expect that the results produced by the
BDCM
and
HPR
procedures can offer insights that will aid mathematicians in devising formal proofs of the “power of few”
[
TV20
,
BD22
,
SS25
]
behavior. This could be facilitated by the fact that the replica symmetric entropy in this problem is equal to the annealed entropy that is typically simpler to treat rigorously.

Appendix A
Derivation of the BDCM relations

Factor graph and BDCM equations.

In the main text, we have defined a probability distribution over graph trajectories

\mathbf{x}

in equation (
2
). We have also shown how this probability distribution factorizes into (
7
) by leveraging the locality of the dynamical rule
f_{i}:S\times S^{|\partial i|}\rightarrow S
and the observables
\tilde{\Xi}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i}):S^{p+c}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
\bar{\Xi}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j}):S^{2(p+c)}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. We repeat the factorized form of the
backtracking dynamical cavity method (BDCM)
probability distribution here for convenience:

P(\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt})=\frac{1}{Z}\prod_{i\in V}\underbrace{\left[e^{-\sum_{\tilde{k}}\lambda_{\tilde{k}}\tilde{\Xi}_{\tilde{k}}(\hbox{\hskip 2.26736pt\hskip-2.26736pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.26736pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.26736pt}_{i})}\mathds{1}\left[x_{i}^{p+1}=f_{i}(x_{i}^{p+c},\mathbf{x}^{p+c}_{\partial i})\right]\prod_{t=1}^{p+c-1}\mathds{1}\left[x_{i}^{t+1}=f_{i}(x_{i}^{t},\mathbf{x}^{t}_{\partial i})\right]\right]}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}(\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.487pt\hskip-1.487pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-1.487pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.487pt}_{\partial i})}\prod_{(ij)\in E}\underbrace{\left[e^{-\sum_{\bar{k}}\lambda_{\bar{k}}\bar{\Xi}_{\bar{k}}(\hbox{\hskip 2.26736pt\hskip-2.26736pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.26736pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.26736pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.26736pt\hskip-2.26736pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.26736pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.26736pt}_{j})}\right]}_{a(\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j})}\,.

(30)

Now, we describe how we can graphically represent this probability distribution and arrive at the
BDCM
equations (
8
), which are used to obtain an estimate of the free entropy density
\Phi=\log Z/n
. We recall that
Z
is the normalization/partition function such that
P(\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt})
is a proper probability distribution.

We start by assuming that the graph
G
is a
tree
and construct a naive
factor graph
, where the node values
\{\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i}\},i\in V
are present as
variable nodes
, and the functions
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt}_{\partial i})\},i\in V
and
\{a(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})\},(ij)\in E
are represented as
factor nodes
. In the factor graph, there is an edge between a variable node and a factor node if and only if the variable
\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i}
is among the arguments of the corresponding factor function. Hence, the factor graph is bipartite. This straightforward construction of the factor graph is depicted in Figure
4
(middle). We can see that the factor graph constructed in this way does not preserve the tree structure of the original graph
G
. This construction introduces many short cycles, which is generally problematic for
belief propagation (BP)
procedures. We remedy this by introducing new variable nodes, the pairs of trajectories
\{(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})\},(ij)\in E
. Now, in place of
edges
in the original graph
G
, we have variable
nodes

\{(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})\}
and their corresponding factor nodes
\{a(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})\}
, which are attached only to these variable nodes. In place of the nodes of
G
, we have the factor nodes
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}(\hbox{\hskip 2.26736pt\hskip-2.26736pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-2.26736pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.26736pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.38957pt\hskip-2.38957pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-2.38957pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.38957pt}_{\partial i})\}
. Hence, we refer to this construction as the factor graph in the
edge-dual
representation of the graph
G
. We denote by
ij=ji
the variable nodes
(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})
, and by
\partial ij
a set of factor nodes that share an edge with the variable node
ij
,
except
the factor node
a(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})
. Hence,
\partial ij
is composed of the two factor nodes
\mathcal{A}_{i}(\hbox{\hskip 2.26736pt\hskip-2.26736pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-2.26736pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.26736pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.38957pt\hskip-2.38957pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-2.38957pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.38957pt}_{\partial i})
and
\mathcal{A}_{j}(\hbox{\hskip 2.26736pt\hskip-2.26736pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-2.26736pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.26736pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 2.38957pt\hskip-2.38957pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-2.38957pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.38957pt}_{\partial j})
. The factor nodes
\mathcal{A}_{i}(\hbox{\hskip 2.26736pt\hskip-2.26736pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-2.26736pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.26736pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.38957pt\hskip-2.38957pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-2.38957pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.38957pt}_{\partial i})
are denoted as
A_{i}
, and we write as
\partial A_{i}
the set of variable nodes on which the factor node acts. We summarize this in Table
4
.

Table 4:
The factor graph is a bipartite graph with variable and factor nodes. This table summarizes the definitions and abbreviations used in the
BDCM
context.

This construction of the factor graph preserves the tree structure of
G
, which plays an important role in the procedure for iterative computation of the normalization
Z
.

(\hbox{\hskip 1.00017pt\hskip-1.00017pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.00017pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{2.58331pt}{0.1507pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.00017pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.00017pt\hskip-1.00017pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.00017pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{2.58331pt}{0.1507pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.00017pt}_{l})

Figure 4:
Factor graph construction for the
BDCM
probability distribution.
(Left.)
Part of the original tree graph
G
.
(Middle.)
Naive construction of the factor graph, where variable nodes (circles) correspond directly to the nodes of
G
. The addition of factor nodes in this manner produces short cycles which is problematic for the
BP
procedure.
(Right.)
Factor graph in the edge-dual representation of the graph
G
with pairs of trajectories
(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})
as variable nodes. This construction preserves the tree structure.

Choosing a factor node
\mathcal{A}_{i}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt}_{\partial i})
for arbitrary
i\in G
and rooting the factor graph in it, we can write the normalization
Z
as

\begin{split}Z&=\sum_{\{\hbox{\hskip 1.487pt\hskip-1.487pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-1.487pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.487pt}\}}P(\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt})=\sum_{\{\hbox{\hskip 1.487pt\hskip-1.487pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-1.487pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.487pt}\}_{G}}\,\prod_{i\in G}\mathcal{A}_{i}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt}_{\partial i})\prod_{(ij)\in G}a(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})=\\
&=\sum_{\{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.487pt\hskip-1.487pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-1.487pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.487pt}_{\partial i}\}}\mathcal{A}_{i}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt}_{\partial i})\prod_{ij\in\partial A_{i}}\left(\sum_{\{\hbox{\hskip 1.487pt\hskip-1.487pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-1.487pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.487pt}\}_{G_{ij}}\backslash\{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}\}}\,\prod_{k\in G_{ij}}\mathcal{A}_{k}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{k},\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt}_{\partial k})\prod_{(kl)\in G_{ij}}a(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{k},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{l})\right)\,.\end{split}

(31)

In the last equality, we use the tree structure of the factor graph to write the rest of the partition function as a product over the tree branches that stem from
\mathcal{A}_{i}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt}_{\partial i})
. We denote these neighboring branches as
\{G_{ij}\},ij\in\partial A_{i}
, when the branch
starts
with the variable node
(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})
(it does not include
A_{i}
).
\{\hbox{\hskip 2.38957pt\hskip-2.38957pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-2.38957pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.38957pt}\}_{G_{ij}}
designates the ensemble of all graph trajectories restricted to the subgraph
G_{ij}
. For a graphical depiction of this, see Figure
5
. Note that what truly makes these branches independent is the fact that the values of the variable nodes
(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})
, for
ij\in\partial A_{i}
are fixed – the sum over these values is in front of the entire product. The rest of the values of the variable nodes in
G_{ij}
, i.e.
\{\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt}\}_{G_{ij}}\backslash\{\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j}\}
, can be summed independently in all branches
ij\in\partial A_{i}
. We refer to the independent sums through these branches – taken with the fixed variable node value
(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})
in (
31
) – as
partial partition functions
, and denote them by
V^{ij\rightarrow A_{i}}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}}
. Thus, we write (
31
) as

Z=\sum_{\{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.487pt\hskip-1.487pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-1.487pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.487pt}_{\partial i}\}}\mathcal{A}_{i}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt}_{\partial i})\prod_{ij\in\partial A_{i}}V^{ij\rightarrow A_{i}}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}}\,.

(32)

Note that
V^{ij\rightarrow A_{i}}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}}
“goes” from a variable node to a factor node. The subscript indicates the fixed value of the variable node
(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})
.
Let us examine the structure of the partial partition functions
V^{ij\rightarrow A_{i}}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}}
. We can take the factor node
a(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})
outside the sum because the values of the variable node
ij
are fixed:

V^{ij\rightarrow A_{i}}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}}=a(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})\hskip-14.22636pt\sum_{\{\hbox{\hskip 1.487pt\hskip-1.487pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-1.487pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.487pt}\}_{G_{ij}}\backslash\{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}\}}\,\prod_{k\in G_{ij}}\mathcal{A}_{k}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{k},\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt}_{\partial k})\prod_{(kl)\in G_{ij}\backslash(ij)}a(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{k},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{l})\,.

(33)

By doing so, we recognize that we are summing only over variable node values of a graph rooted at
A_{j}
(see Figure
5
). We denote this graph rooted at
A_{j}
by
G_{A_{j}}
, and introduce another partial partition function going from factor node to variable node with fixed variable node value
(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})
:

R^{A_{j}\rightarrow ij}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i}}=\sum_{\{\hbox{\hskip 1.487pt\hskip-1.487pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-1.487pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.487pt}\}_{G_{A_{j}}}}\,\prod_{k\in G_{A_{j}}}\mathcal{A}_{k}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{k},\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt}_{\partial k})\prod_{(kl)\in G_{A_{j}}}a(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{k},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{l})\,.

(34)

We immediately have

V^{ij\rightarrow A_{i}}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}}=a(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})R^{A_{j}\rightarrow ij}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i}}\,.

(35)

Note that the values of
(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})
in
R^{A_{j}\rightarrow ij}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i}}
are present in the factor node
A_{j}
, in which we rooted the graph
G_{A_{j}}
. Additionally, the fixed value of
\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j}
also partially fixes the variable nodes
(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{k},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})=kj\in A_{j}\backslash ij
. Therefore, similarly as above for
Z
, we write
R^{A_{j}\rightarrow ij}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i}}
as

R^{A_{j}\rightarrow ij}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i}}=\sum_{\{\hbox{\hskip 1.487pt\hskip-1.487pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-1.487pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.487pt}_{\partial j\backslash i}\}}\mathcal{A}_{j}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt}_{\partial j})\prod_{jk\in\partial A_{j}\backslash ij}\,\left(a(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{k})\sum_{\{\hbox{\hskip 1.487pt\hskip-1.487pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-1.487pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.487pt}\}_{G_{jk}}\backslash\{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{k}\}}\,\prod_{\hskip 2.84544ptl\in G_{jk}}\mathcal{A}_{l}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{l},\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt}_{\partial l})\prod_{(lm)\in G_{jk}\backslash(jk)}a(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{l},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{m})\right)\,,

(36)

where
G_{jk}
is defined as before with
G_{ij}
and is also depicted in Figure
5
. We recognize (
33
) in (
36
) and get

R^{A_{j}\rightarrow ij}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i}}=\sum_{\{\hbox{\hskip 1.487pt\hskip-1.487pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-1.487pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.487pt}_{\partial j\backslash i}\}}\mathcal{A}_{j}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt}_{\partial j})\prod_{jk\in\partial A_{j}\backslash ij}V^{jk\rightarrow A_{j}}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{k}}\,.

(37)

Substitution of (
37
) into (
35
) results in an iterative scheme for obtaining values of the partial partition functions
V
:

V^{ij\rightarrow A_{i}}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}}=a(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})\sum_{\{\hbox{\hskip 1.487pt\hskip-1.487pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-1.487pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.487pt}_{\partial j\backslash i}\}}\mathcal{A}_{j}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt}_{\partial j})\prod_{jk\in\partial A_{j}\backslash ij}V^{jk\rightarrow A_{j}}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{k}}\,.

(38)

A_{k}
jk
a_{jk}
A_{j}
ij
a_{ij}
A_{i}

V^{jk\rightarrow A_{j}}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{k}}

R^{A_{j}\rightarrow ij}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i}}

V^{ij\rightarrow A_{i}}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}}

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0}G_{jk}}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}G_{A_{j}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}G_{ij}}

Figure 5:
Derivation of the
BP
scheme in the
BDCM
setting. The tree structure of the factor graph is utilized to rewrite partition function
Z
via the partial partition functions
V
and
R
, for which we obtain the local iterative schemes (
35
) and (
37
).

Partition functions typically grow exponentially with
n
, as they are a sum over an exponential number of terms. Then the iterative scheme (
38
) tends to be numerically unstable and it is better to transition to normalized
messages

\chi^{i\rightarrow j}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}}=\frac{V^{ij\rightarrow A_{j}}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}}}{\sum_{\{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}^{\prime}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}^{\prime}_{j}\}}V^{ij\rightarrow A_{j}}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}^{\prime}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}^{\prime}_{j}}}\,.

(39)

We use the convention that
i\rightarrow j
designates a message that begins in variable node
ij
and goes in the direction of the factor node
A_{j}
. Thus, within this convention, we refer only to the nodes of the original graph
G
. The message
\chi^{i\rightarrow j}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}}
is the probability that the variable node
ij
takes the value
\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j}
when restricted to subgraph
G_{ji}
(the subgraph does not include
A_{j}
). This is the probability in the presence of a cavity – hence the name cavity method
[
KZ21
]
.

We use the iterative scheme for the
V
s to write

\begin{split}\chi^{i\rightarrow j}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{mes def}\&\eqref{BDCM eqs for V}}}{{=}}\frac{a(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})\sum_{\{\hbox{\hskip 1.487pt\hskip-1.487pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-1.487pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.487pt}_{\partial i\backslash j}\}}\mathcal{A}_{i}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt}_{\partial i})\prod_{ik\in\partial A_{i}\backslash ij}V^{ik\rightarrow A_{i}}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{k},\hbox{\hskip (40)

Writing
ik\in A_{i}\backslash ij
as
k\in\partial i\backslash j
, we obtain the
