.

C.3
Dynamic programming scheme for HPR

We show how dynamic programming significantly reduces the algorithmic complexity of Algorithm
1
for the
HPR
procedure. We repeat the general
HPR
message update equation here for convenience:

\chi^{i\rightarrow j}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}}=\frac{1}{Z^{i\rightarrow j}}\,a(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})\sum_{\hbox{\hskip 1.487pt\hskip-1.487pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-1.487pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.487pt}_{\partial i\backslash j}}\mathcal{A}_{i}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt}_{\partial i})\!\!\prod_{k\in\partial i\backslash j}\!b^{k}_{x^{1}_{k}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi^{k\rightarrow i}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{k},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i}}\,.

(79)

The factor node
\mathcal{A}_{i}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt}_{\partial i})
depends on the neighboring trajectories of node
i
only via the dynamical rule
f_{i}(x_{i},\mathbf{x}_{\partial i})
. In the following, we consider a dynamical rule that depends only on the sum of the neighboring values:
f_{i}(x_{i},\mathbf{x}_{\partial i})=f_{i}(x_{i},x_{j},\varrho_{\partial i\backslash j})
, where
\varrho_{\partial i\backslash j}=\sum_{k\in\partial i\backslash j}x_{k}
. This is the case for the majority dynamics rule (
1
) from Section
4
. Thus, we can write the majority dynamics rule as:

f_{i}(x_{i},x_{j},\varrho_{\partial i\backslash j})=\mathrm{sign}\biggl(2(x_{j}+\varrho_{\partial i\backslash j}))+x_{i}\biggr)\,.

(80)

Hence, for the factor node
\mathcal{A}_{i}
we can write

\mathcal{A}_{i}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt}_{\partial i})=\mathcal{A}_{i}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\partial i\backslash j})\,,

(81)

where
\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\partial i\backslash j}
is the
sum trajectory

(\varrho^{1}_{\partial i\backslash j},\dots,\varrho^{T}_{\partial i\backslash j})
with
\varrho^{t}_{\partial i\backslash j}=\sum_{k\in\partial i\backslash j}x^{t}_{k}
. This is useful because a large number of trajectory combinations correspond to the same value of
\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\partial i\backslash j}
and we can rewrite the message update (
79
) as

\chi^{i\rightarrow j}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}}=\frac{1}{Z^{i\rightarrow j}}\,a(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})\sum_{\hbox{\hskip 1.26668pt\hskip-1.26668pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varrho$}\hskip-1.26668pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.95277pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.26668pt}_{\partial i\backslash j}}\mathcal{A}_{i}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\partial i\backslash j})\underbrace{\sum_{\hbox{\hskip 1.487pt\hskip-1.487pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-1.487pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.487pt}_{\partial i\backslash j}}\mathds{1}\left[\sum_{k\in\partial i\backslash j}\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{k}=\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\partial i\backslash j}\right]\prod_{k\in\partial i\backslash j}\!b^{k}_{x^{1}_{k}}\chi^{k\rightarrow i}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{k},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i}}}_{L_{\tilde{D}=d(i)-1}(\hbox{\hskip 2.26736pt\hskip-2.26736pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.26736pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.26736pt}_{i},\,\hbox{\hskip 2.0745pt\hskip-2.0745pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.0745pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.3611pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.0745pt}_{\partial i\backslash j})}\,,

(82)

where
L_{\tilde{D}}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\,\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\partial i\backslash j})
can be computed recursively. We start with
\tilde{D}=1
, when

L_{1}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{1})=b^{k_{1}}_{x^{1}_{k_{1}}}\chi^{k_{1}\rightarrow i}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{k_{1}}=\hbox{\hskip 1.26668pt\hskip-1.26668pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varrho$}\hskip-1.26668pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.95277pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.26668pt}_{1},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i}}\,,

(83)

for some
k_{1}\in\partial i\backslash j
. Indeed, we aim to obtain the sum of the products of the biased neighboring messages,
L_{\tilde{D}=d(i)-1}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\,\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\partial i\backslash j})
, but we start with just one message, and hence for the sum trajectory of neighboring “nodes” we have directly

\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{1}=\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{k_{1}}
. Then, we add neighboring messages according to

L_{\tilde{D}}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\tilde{D}})=\sum_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{k_{\tilde{D}}}}\sum_{\hbox{\hskip 1.26668pt\hskip-1.26668pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varrho$}\hskip-1.26668pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.95277pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.26668pt}_{\tilde{D}-1}}\mathds{1}\left[\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{k_{\tilde{D}}}+\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\tilde{D}-1}=\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\tilde{D}}\right]L_{\tilde{D}-1}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\tilde{D}-1})\,\,b^{k_{\tilde{D}}}_{x^{1}_{k_{\tilde{D}}}}\chi^{k_{\tilde{D}}\rightarrow i}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{k_{\tilde{D}}},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i}}\,.

(84)

In this way, we use all neighboring nodes in
\partial i\backslash j
uniquely for the
final

\tilde{D}=d(i)-1
. Note that we use (
84
) to obtain
L_{\tilde{D}}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\tilde{D}})
for each combination of
\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\tilde{D}}
.

In the case of
d
-
RRGs
the final value of
D=d(i)-1
is the same for every node,
d(i)=d\,\forall i\in V
. Hence, we need to compute and keep only the final values
L_{\tilde{D}=d-1}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\,\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\partial i\backslash j})
for each message (see Algorithm
2
for the algorithmic procedure in this case). If the node degree varies, we can use the same dynamic procedure as described here, but we need to store
L_{\tilde{D}=d(i)-1}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\,\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\partial i\backslash j})
for the given range of node degrees.

The new update scheme

\chi^{i\rightarrow j}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}}=\frac{1}{Z^{i\rightarrow j}}\,a(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j})\sum_{\hbox{\hskip 1.26668pt\hskip-1.26668pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varrho$}\hskip-1.26668pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.95277pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.26668pt}_{\partial i\backslash j}}\mathcal{A}_{i}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{j},\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\partial i\backslash j})L_{d(i)-1}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\,\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\partial i\backslash j})

(85)

is summing over
d(i)^{p+c}
terms (the sum of
d(i)-1
binary values can result in
d(i)
outcomes) instead of
2^{(d-1)(p+c)}
terms. Hence, the exponential dependence on
d(i)
is removed. Naturally, a similar dynamic programming scheme can also be devised for other permutation-symmetric dynamical rules.

Algorithm 2

H
istory
P
assing
R
einforcement – dynamic programming for majority dynamics on
d
-
RRGs

(factor graph
(G,S,F,\{\Xi_{k}\},\{\lambda_{k}\},p,c),
steps threshold
T_{\rm{max}},
damping
\varepsilon,
hyperparameters:
\pi,\gamma
)

Initialize uniformly at random biases
b^{i}_{x_{i}^{1}}
and messages
\chi^{i\rightarrow j}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}}
on the factor graph and normalize them.

Initialize the sum trajectory values
\{\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{1}\}
with single node trajectory values
\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}\in S^{p+c}
, as
\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{1}=\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}
.

t\leftarrow 0

while

t\leq T_{\rm{max}}

do

Compute
L_{1}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{1})
for all
\{\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i}\}
and
\{\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{1}\}
according to (
83
).

for

\tilde{D}\in\{2,\dots,d-1\}

do

For all
\{\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\tilde{D}}\}
and
\{\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i}\}
compute
L_{\tilde{D}}(\hbox{\hskip 2.85764pt\hskip-2.85764pt\hbox{$x$}\hskip-2.85764pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.85764pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 2.58507pt\hskip-2.58507pt\hbox{$\varrho$}\hskip-2.58507pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.94444pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.58507pt}_{\tilde{D}})
according to (
84
).

end

for

Update all the messages
\chi^{i\rightarrow j}_{\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{i},\hbox{\hskip 1.40024pt\hskip-1.40024pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\hskip-1.40024pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.61664pt}{0.21098pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 1.40024pt}_{j}}
according to (
85
) with damping
\varepsilon
(
29
).

Compute the marginals
\mu^{i}_{x_{i}^{1}=s}
from eq. (
25
).

Update each bias independently according to (
24
) with probability
p(t)
(
27
).

Obtain new trial solution
\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{sol}}_{t}
using (
28
).

t\leftarrow t+1

end

while

return

\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{sol}}_{t}
with lowest initial magnetization and which is a solution.

Appendix D
Simulated Annealing

In this section, we present the
simulated annealing (SA)

[
KGV83
]
procedure for enhancing consensus in our setting. That is, we use
SA
to obtain initializations of
d
-
RRGs
leading to all-ones attractor via majority dynamics while minimizing
m_{\mathrm{init}}
. We compare the results of this widely used Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) optimization method
[
Met+53
,
Has70
,
KGV83
]
with the
HPR
algorithm in Section
5
.

We define the energy function

E(\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt})=anm_{\mathrm{init}}(\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt})-bnm_{\mathrm{attr}}(\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt})\,,

(86)

which we aim to minimize.
n
is the number of nodes and we introduce two parameters
a
and
b
, which are interpretable as two inverse temperatures. The energy function (
86
) aligns with our goal of finding initializations where minority becomes majority via majority dynamics: its minima are the states that minimize
m_{\mathrm{init}}
and maximize
m_{\mathrm{attr}}
(recall that homogeneous attractor corresponds to
m_{\mathrm{attr}}=1
). Note that the energy is a function only of the graph initialization because of the deterministic dynamics:
E(\hbox{\hskip 3.0347pt\hskip-3.0347pt\hbox{$\mathbf{x}$}\hskip-3.0347pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{5.16663pt}{0.3014pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 3.0347pt})=anm(\mathbf{x}^{1})-bnm(\mathbf{x}^{p+1})=anm(\mathbf{x}^{1})-bnm(F^{\circ p}(\mathbf{x^{1}}))=E(\mathbf{x}^{1})
. This is convenient because the combinatorial space to search is
S^{n}
instead of
S^{n(p+1)}
. We construct the Boltzmann probability measure on graph configurations
\mathbf{x}^{1}=\mathbf{x}\in S^{n}

P(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{Z}e^{-E(\mathbf{x})}\,.

(87)

As we “cool” the system, i.e. we increase the inverse temperatures
a\sim b\rightarrow\infty
, the measure (
87
) concentrates on the ground-states of the energy (
86
). Hence, if we can sample from this probability distribution at lower and lower temperatures, we obtain (approximate) ground-states of the energy (
86
). The
SA
procedure achieves this by performing Metropolis-Hastings (MH) steps with increasing inverse temperatures. This MCMC process is defined by the transition rates based on the ratio
P(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})/P(\mathbf{x})=e^{-(E(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})-E(\mathbf{x}))}
. If the probability of state
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
is higher than the probability of
\mathbf{x}
(hence
E(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})<E(\mathbf{x})
) the MH step always accepts the new state
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
. If the state
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
is less probable, MH accepts it only with probability
e^{-(E(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})-E(\mathbf{x}))}
. Thus, the transition rates from state
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
is

p(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}|\mathbf{x})=\mathrm{min}\left(1,e^{-(E(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})-E(\mathbf{x}))}\right)\,,

(88)

which satisfy the detailed balance condition with the equilibrium distribution given by (
87
). Moreover, by defining the transition rates by a ratio of probabilities (
87
) we get rid of the hard to compute partition function
Z
.

A slow cooling is essential for a good performance of the
SA
procedure. We adopt the following cooling schedule. We start with inverse temperature values
a_{i},b_{i}
and increase them with the same rate
\alpha>1
, so that
a^{t+1}=\alpha a^{t}
(and equivalently for
b^{t+1}
) until we reach the final inverse temperature values
a_{f},b_{f}
. We chose the numerical values of
\{a_{i},b_{i},a_{f},b_{f},\alpha\}
based on empirical observations of the performance of the
SA
algorithm in our setting. The precise values used for the results in Table
3
are summarized in Table
9
. We initialize the binary node values uniformly at random and the trial state
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
is created by choosing one node (uniformly at random) and flipping its sign. The entire
SA
procedure is summarized in Algorithm
3
.

Algorithm 3

S
imulated
A
nnealing
(G,S,F,E,\alpha,a_{i},a_{f},b_{i},b_{f},T_{\rm{max}}
)

Initialize graph configuration
\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{sol}}\in S^{n}
uniformly at random and compute the energy
E(\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{sol}})
, with
a=a_{i}
,
b=b_{i}
, according to (
86
).

t\leftarrow 0

while

\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{sol}}
is not a solution
and

t\leq T_{\rm{max}}

do

Choose
i\in V
uniformly at random and take
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}=-\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{sol}}_{i}
,
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{j}=\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{sol}}_{j}
for all
j\in V\backslash i
.

\Delta E\leftarrow E(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})-E(\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{sol}})
.

r\leftarrow\rm{Random}(0,1)

if

r\leq\rm{min}(1,e^{-\Delta E})

then

\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{sol}}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

end

if

if

a<a_{f}

then

a\leftarrow\alpha a

end

if

if

b<b_{f}

then

b\leftarrow\alpha b

end

if

t\leftarrow t+1

end

while

d

for

. The cooling rate was set to
\alpha=1.0005
, and we used
T_{\mathrm{max}}=2n^{3}
for the maximum
SA
steps threshold.

Figure 15:
Values of
m_{\rm init}(\mathbf{x}^{\rm sol}_{t})
and the time to consensus
T_{\rm eff}(\mathbf{x}^{\rm sol}_{t})
under the majority rule, for all
\mathbf{x}^{\rm sol}_{t}
from the trajectories of SA runs with
d=3,4,5,6
. We exclude every
\mathbf{x}^{\rm sol}_{t}
that failed to reach consensus within
28
time-steps. To avoid memory issues during the long runs of the SA procedure we plot the following combination of the SA runs. We ran the SA until we reach a solution (
T_{\rm eff}=p
) for 10 random graphs and plot every
10^{4}
th
(m_{\mathrm{init}}(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\mathrm{sol}}),T_{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\mathrm{sol}}))
pair. And we ran the SA for another 10 random graphs for
10^{7}
time-steps and plot all
(m_{\mathrm{init}}(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\mathrm{sol}}),T_{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\mathrm{sol}}))
pairs without reaching the solution. This allows us to resolve the early-stage behavior in detail while also capturing the final magnetization values reached, where
m_{\rm init}(\mathbf{x}^{\rm sol}_{t})
fluctuates only weakly around its final value. The black lines are the lowest
m_{\rm init}
for each
T_{\rm eff}>p
with
p=1,2,3
over all runs and black dots indicate the best
m_{\rm init}
for a given
T_{\rm eff}=p
. Note that this figure reports different values than Table
3
, where we reported the average best initialization. The hyperparameter values are
a_{i}=0.015
,
b_{i}=0.01
,
a_{f}=4.5
and
b_{f}=5
, except for the case
d=3
,
n=1000
where
b_{f}=6
was used. The cooling rate was set to
\alpha=1.0005
, and for the maximum
SA
steps threshold we used
T_{\mathrm{max}}=n^{3}
and
T_{\mathrm{max}}=10^{7}
.

We note that the behavior of the
m_{\mathrm{init}}(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\mathrm{sol}})
trajectories during the SA run depicted in Figure
15
depends on the chosen cooling mechanism. In addition, their behavior in finding solutions with
T_{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\mathrm{sol}})>p
differs somewhat from that of the HPR trajectories shown in Figure
12
. For instance, the increase in the initial magnetization observed for
p<T_{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\mathrm{sol}})<12
in the cases
p=3
,
d=5,6
, and
n=500,1000
might be attributed to the fact that the temperature regime where the term in
a
is better optimized than the term in
b
does not persist long enough to generate these solutions. Furthermore, the solid lines corresponding to different values of
p
tend to collapse onto a single curve at large
T_{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\mathrm{sol}})
. This behavior may stem from the structure of the energy function (
86
) and the chosen cooling parameters: the first term is identical for all
p
, while the second term exerts a similar influence on the magnetization at high
T_{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\mathrm{sol}})
for
p=1,2,3
. We did not investigate these effects further, as this cooling regime empirically proved effective for finding solutions with
T_{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\mathrm{sol}})\leq p
, which was our primary focus.
